\alpha\in\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])
at points of
{Y}_{0}(2)
valued in local fields. By the below result, when pulled back to a suitable local field, the triviality of cyclic algebras can be understood in terms of classical Hilbert symbol.

Notation 5.8
.

Let
K
be a local field containing a primitive
n
th root of unity
\zeta
. The choice of
\zeta
defines an isomorphism
\underline{C_{n}}_{K}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mu_{n,K}
. Combined with the usual Kummer isomorphism, we have
H^{1}(K,C_{n})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}(K,\mu_{n})\simeq K^{\times}/(K^{\times})^{n}
. Given
a,b\in K^{\times}
, let
(a,b)_{\zeta}\in\Br(K)
denote the image of
a\otimes b
under the composition

\frac{K^{\times}}{(K^{\times})^{n}}\otimes\frac{K^{\times}}{(K^{\times})^{n}}\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}(K,C_{n})\otimes H^{1}(K,\mu_{n})\xrightarrow{\smile}H^{2}(K,\mu_{n})\to H^{2}(K,\mathbx G_{m})=\Br(K).

Proposition 5.9

(
[
AM20
, Proposition 2.17]
)
.

Let
K
be a local field containing a primitive
n
th root of unity
\zeta
. Let
\mathfrak{p}
denote the prime ideal in the valuation ring of
K
, and let

\!\left(\frac{-,-}{\mathfrak{p}}\right):\frac{K^{\times}}{(K^{\times})^{n}}\otimes\frac{K^{\times}}{(K^{\times})^{n}}\longrightarrow\mu_{n}(K)

denote the usual Hilbert symbol
[
Neu99
, Section V.3]
. Then, for any
a,b\in K^{\times}

(a,b)_{\zeta}=1\in\Br(K)\iff\!\left(\frac{a,b}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=1\in\mu_{n}(K).

We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[\frac{1}{2p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

with
\sigma
the section computed in Theorem
5.7
. We are interested in figuring out which Brauer classes in the bottom row come from ones in the top row. We start by determining when the elements of
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])
coming from
H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p})
do not extend to
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2))
.

Remark 5.10
.

Our earlier computation (Lemma
4.5
) that
H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p})\simeq\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p}
shows that, for every
n
, there is a unique Galois extension
F_{n}/\mathbx Q
which is unramified outside
2p
and whose Galois group is isomorphic to
\mathbx Z/p^{n}\mathbx Z
. In fact, because
\mathbx Q(\zeta_{p^{n+1}})/\mathbx Q
has (cyclic) Galois group
\!\left(\mathbx Z/p^{n+1}\mathbx Z\right)^{\times}
and is unramified outside
p
, we see that
F_{n}
is the unique subfield of
\mathbx Q(\zeta_{p^{n+1}})
with Galois group
\mathbx Z/p^{n}\mathbx Z
over
\mathbx Q
. Thus,
H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],C_{p^{n}})=H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p})[p^{n}]
is generated by the class of the ring of integers of
F_{n}/\mathbx Q
.

Definition 5.11
.

We define
\alpha_{n}:=[O_{F_{n}}]
to be the class of the ring of integers of
F_{n}/\mathbx Q
in
H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],C_{p^{n}})
.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
K=K_{n}=\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p^{n}})
,
\mathfrak{p}
the prime ideal in the valuation ring of
K
,
\alpha_{n}\in H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],C_{p^{n}})
as in Definition
5.11
, and
t_{0}\in O_{K}
such that
E:y^{2}=x(x-1)(x-t_{0})
has good reduction at
\mathfrak{p}
(i.e. is a Legendre curve over
O_{K}
) and

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},t_{0}/(t_{0}-1)^{2}}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\neq 1.

Let
f:\Spec O_{K}\to{Y}_{0}(2)
be the map given by
E
, and
f_{K}:\Spec K\to{Y}_{0}(2)[1/p]
the pullback of the map to the generic fiber. Then
f_{K}^{*}(\sigma(\alpha_{n}))\neq 0
, where
\sigma
is the section computed in Theorem
5.7
, and thus
\sigma(\alpha_{n})
does not extend to
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2))
.

Proof.

We first explicitly compute the class of
\alpha_{n}
restricted to
K
. Since
K
contains a primitive
p^{n}
th root of unity,

H^{1}(K,C_{p^{n}})=H^{1}(K,\mu_{p^{n}})=\mathbx G_{m}(K)/p^{n}=\frac{K^{\times}}{(K^{\times})^{p^{n}}},

where
x\in K^{\times}
corresponds to the class of the extension
K\!\left(\sqrt[p^{n}]{x}\right)/K
in
H^{1}(K,C_{p^{n}})
.

The class of
\alpha_{n}
, the extension
F_{n}
over
\mathbx Q
, restricted to
K
is given by the class of the extension
KF_{n}
over
K
. The compositum
KF_{n}
is equal to
\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p^{n+1}})
, because
KF_{n}\subset\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p^{n+1}})
by construction and
[KF_{n}:\mathbx Q_{p}]
is divisible by both
p^{n}
and
(p-1)
, again by construction. Since
\zeta_{p^{n+1}}=\sqrt[p^{n}]{\zeta_{p}}
, we conclude that
\alpha_{n}=[F_{n}]\in H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],C_{p^{n}})
pulls back to
\zeta_{p}\in K^{\times}/(K^{\times})^{p^{n}}\cong H^{1}(K,C_{p^{n}})
.

We have the following commutative diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)_{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(K,C_{p^{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[\frac{1}{2p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],C_{p^{n}}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t=t_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

in which
\sigma
is the section in Theorem
5.7
, and the vertical arrows up are restrictions to
K
. The vertical map on the right is given by
\alpha_{n}\mapsto\zeta_{p}
, and
f_{K_{n}}^{*}
is given by the composition
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[\frac{1}{2p}])\to{}\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}(K_{n})
. Thus
f_{K_{n}}^{*}(\sigma(\alpha_{n}))
is given by the cup product
(\zeta_{p},t_{0}/(t_{0}-1)^{2})
, which is nontrivial since

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},t_{0}/(t_{0}-1)^{2}}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\neq 1.

Therefore using the strategy in Remark
5.2
, we conclude that
\sigma(\alpha_{n})
does not extend to
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2))
.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

If
\alpha_{1}
, which generates
H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p})[p]
, does
not
extend over
{Y}_{0}(2)
, e.g., we find suitable
t
making

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},t/(t-1)^{2}}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\neq 1,

where
\mathfrak{p}
is the prime of
K_{1}=\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})
– then in fact no nonzero
\alpha\in H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p})
will extend over
{Y}_{0}(2)
. Indeed, suppose some such
\alpha
does. Then all of its multiples extend over
{Y}_{0}(2)
as well. However,
\alpha
will be of order
p^{n}
for some
n\geq 1
, so
p^{n-1}\alpha
will be a generator for the
p
-torsion in
H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p})\cong\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p}
. Thus,
\alpha_{1}=up^{n-1}\alpha
for some
u
from which we would deduce that
\alpha_{1}
extends over
{Y}_{0}(2)
, a contradiction.

Thus, we arrive at the task of computing some Hilbert symbols over
K_{1}=\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})
. Let
\mathfrak{p}=(\zeta_{p}-1)\subset\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]=O_{K_{1}}
be its maximal ideal.

Remark 5.14
.

Consider any
a\in O_{\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})}=\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
such that
a\equiv 1\pmod{\mathfrak{p}}
. Then,
[
Neu99
, Proposition V.3.8]
tells us that

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},a}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=\zeta_{p}^{\Tr(\log a)/p}\in\mu_{p}(\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})),

where
\Tr=\Tr_{\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})/\mathbx Q_{p}}
and
\log
is the usual
p
-adic logarithm:

\log(1-x)=-\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{x^{n}}{n}.

Proposition 5.15
.

There exists some
t\in\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]^{\times}=O^{\times}_{K_{1}}
such that
\Delta(t):=16t^{2}(t-1)^{2}\in\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]^{\times}
(i.e.
E_{t}:y^{2}=x(x-1)(x-t)
is a Legendre curve over
\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
) and

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},t/(t-1)^{2}}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\neq 1\in\mu_{p}.

Proof.

Consider some
a,m\in\mathbx Z
with
p\nmid am
, and observe that

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},m^{p}+ap}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},1+am^{-p}p}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},m^{p}}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},1+am^{-p}p}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=\zeta^{-am^{-p}}_{p}=\zeta_{p}^{-am^{-1}}\neq 1,

with the second-to-last equality following from Remark
5.14
, because
\Tr_{\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})/\mathbx Q_{p}}
is multiplication by
p-1
on
\mathbx Q_{p}
. Thus, we may construct our desired
t\in\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
by solving
t(t-1)^{-2}=m^{p}+ap
.

In general, the solution of
t(t-1)^{-2}=c
is

(10)

t=\frac{(2c+1)\pm\sqrt{4c+1}}{2c}.

In our case,
c=m^{p}+ap
which is a unit
\pmod{p}
, so
2c
is invertible in
\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
.

Thus this is solvable exactly when
4(m^{p}+ap)+1
is a square in
\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
. By Hensel’s lemma, this will hold when
4(m^{p}+ap)+1\equiv 4m+1\pmod{p}
is a nonzero square mod
p
, and in fact, working mod
p
will imply there is a solution in
\mathbx Z_{p}
.

•

If
p>3
, we may choose
m\in\mathbx Z
so that
m\equiv 3\cdot 4^{-1}\pmod{p}
(and choose
a
arbitrarily as long as
p\nmid a
), and then a
t
satisfying
t/(t-1)^{2}=m^{p}+ap
is guaranteed to exist.

We also need to check that
\Delta(t)=16t^{2}(t-1)^{2}\in\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]^{\times}
. Because
t/(t-1)^{2}=m^{p}+ap=:c
is a unit, this is equivalent to
t
being a unit. By (
10
),
t
is a unit if and only if
(2c+1)\pm\sqrt{4c+1}
is nonzero modulo
\mathfrak{p}
. Since
4c+1
is a nonzero square mod
\mathfrak{p}
, the expression
(2c+1)\pm\sqrt{4c+1}
takes on two distinct values mod
\mathfrak{p}
, and so at least one of them will be nonzero mod
\mathfrak{p}
.

•

If
p=3
, we may take
(a,m)=(1,-1)
so
4(m^{p}+ap)+1=3^{2}
is a square in
\mathbx Z_{3}[\zeta_{3}]
. Hence, there will again exist some
t
satisfying
t/(t-1)^{2}=m^{p}+ap
.

In this case,
t=(5\pm 3)/4
; this is a unit, which makes
\Delta(t)
also a unit, for either possible value.
∎

The above proposition will suffice for showing that classes in the image of

\sigma:H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p})\to\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])

do not extend over
{Y}_{0}(2)
. In general, however, a element of
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])
is a sum of such a class along with one pulled back from
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}])
. Because of this, it will be useful to also have access to Legendre curves over
\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
for which the relevant Hilbert symbol
does
vanish.

Proposition 5.16
.

There exists some
t\in\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]^{\times}=O^{\times}_{K_{1}}
such that
\Delta(t):=16t^{2}(t-1)^{2}\in\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]^{\times}
(i.e.
E_{t}:y^{2}=x(x-1)(x-t)
is a Legendre curve over
\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
) and

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},t/(t-1)^{2}}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=1\in\mu_{p}.

Proof.

In this case, the main observation is that
\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},m^{p}}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=1
for all integers
m
, so we are interested in solving
t/(t-1)^{2}=m^{p}
in
\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
. Suppose
p\nmid m
. Then,
t/(t-1)^{2}=m^{p}
has a solution if and only if
4m^{p}+1
is a square in
\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
. Hensel’s lemma tells us that this will happen if
4m^{p}+1\equiv 4m+1\pmod{p}
is a nonzero square mod
p
. For
p>3
, we may take
m=3\cdot 4^{-1}\pmod{p}
and then argue that we can find a resulting
t
such that
\Delta(t)
is a unit exactly as in the proof of Proposition
5.15
.

If
p=3
, we instead use
[
AM20
, Proof of Lemma 8.3]
which computes that

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{3},t}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=\zeta_{3}^{-2-b-\frac{b^{2}}{2}}\,\text{ and }\,\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{3},t-1}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=\zeta_{3}^{b-\frac{b^{2}}{2}}.

if
t=2+b\pi
, where
\pi:=1-\zeta_{3}\in\mathbx Z_{3}[\zeta_{3}]
. We may take
t=2+2\pi
, so
b=2
, and observe that

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{3},t/(t-1)^{2}}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=\zeta_{3}^{-6}\left/\!\left(\zeta_{3}^{0}\right)^{2}\right.=\zeta^{-6}_{3}=1.

At the same time, both
t
and
t-1
are units in
\mathbx Z_{3}[\zeta_{3}]
, so
\Delta(t)
is as well.
∎

5.2.
Showing No Class in
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])
Extends Over
{Y}_{0}(2)

Let
\sigma
denote the so-named section constructed in Theorem
5.7
.

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
p\geqslant 3
and consider any nonzero
\alpha\in H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p})
. Then,
\sigma(\alpha)\in\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])=\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[\frac{1}{2p}])
does not extend over
{Y}_{0}(2)
, i.e. it is not in the image of
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2))\to\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])
.

Proof.

By Remark
5.13
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\alpha=\alpha_{1}=[O_{F_{1}}]
. By Proposition
5.15
, we may choose some
t\in\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
so that
E:y^{2}=x(x-1)(x-t)
is a Legendre curve over
\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
and so that

(11)

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},t/(t-1)^{2}}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\neq 1\in\mu_{p}(\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})).

The result follows from Proposition
5.12
.
∎

Proposition 5.18
.

Consider any nonzero
\beta\in\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}])\subset\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])
. Then,
\beta
does not extend over
{Y}_{0}(2)
.

Proof.

Remark
4.3
shows that
{Y}_{0}(2)
has a
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-point, say
x:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]\to{Y}_{0}(2)
. Let
x_{p}:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}]\to{Y}_{0}(2)[1/p]
be the corresponding restricted section, and consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}_{0}(2)[1/p]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}_{0}(2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

Commutativity of this diagram shows that

\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}])\cap\operatorname{im}\!\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2))\to\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])\right)\subset\operatorname{im}\!\left(\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}])\to\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}])\right).

Since
\Br^{\prime}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}])=\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z

[
AM20
, Example 2.19(2)]
has no
p
-power torsion, any such
\beta
that extends must be 0.
∎

Proposition 5.19
.

Choose
\alpha\in H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}],\mathbx Q_{p}/\mathbx Z_{p})
and
\beta\in\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}])\subset\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])
such that
\sigma(\alpha)+\beta\in\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)[1/p])
is nonzero. Then,
\sigma(\alpha)+\beta
does not extend over
{Y}_{0}(2)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.16
, we may fix
t\in\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
such that
E:y^{2}=x(x-1)(x-t)
is a Legendre curve over
\mathbx Z_{p}[\zeta_{p}]
and so that

(12)

\!\left(\frac{\zeta_{p},t/(t-1)^{2}}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)=1\in\mu_{p}(\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})).

Let
f:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})\to{Y}_{0}(2)
be the point corresponding to
E
with the usual level 2 structure on Legendre curves. Suppose that
\sigma(\alpha)+\beta

does
extend over
{Y}_{0}(2)
, so then
f^{*}\!\left(\sigma(\alpha)+\beta\right)=0\in\Br\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})
. Because of (
12
), Proposition
5.9
shows that
f^{*}\sigma(\alpha)=0\in\Br\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p})
. We conclude that
f^{*}\beta=0
as well. Since
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}])\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br(\mathbx Q_{p})\hookrightarrow\Br(\mathbx Q_{p}(\zeta_{p}))
is injective (with the first isomorphism following from
[
AM20
, Example 2.19(3) and the discussion above it]
), we conclude that
\beta=0
. Thus,
\sigma(\alpha)
extends over
{Y}_{0}(2)
, but now Proposition
5.17
shows this forces
\sigma(\alpha)=0
.
∎

We have the following corollary, which is Proposition
5.3
, restated for the convenience of the reader.

Corollary 5.20
.

For all primes
p\geqslant 3
,
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.62234pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.19855pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br^{\prime}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{p}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2))=0
.

6.
The 2-primary torsion

6.1.
The 2-Local Spectral Sequence

Notation 6.1
.

Throughout this section
S
will denote a connected, regular noetherian
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-scheme. Furthermore, given an abelian group
M
, we write
M_{(2)}:=M\otimes Z\mathbx Z_{(2)}
, where
\mathbx Z_{(2)}
denotes the integers localized at the prime
(2)
.

The goal of this section is to compute
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})
, the
2
-primary torsion in the Brauer group of
{Y}_{0}(2)
over
S
. As in Section
4
, our main tool for doing so will be the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence associated to the
C_{2}
-cover
{Y}(2)\to{Y}_{0}(2)
. Localized at the prime
(2)
, this is the spectral sequence

E_{2}^{i,j}=H^{i}(C_{2},H^{j}({Y}(2)_{S},\mathbx G_{m}))_{(2)}\implies H^{i+j}({Y}_{0}(2)_{S},\mathbx G_{m})_{(2)}.

The relevant part of its
E_{2}
-page is pictured in Figure
1
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pic(S)_{(2)}\oplus\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pic(S)[2]\oplus\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pic(S)/2\oplus\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx G_{m}(S)_{(2)}\oplus\mathbx Z_{(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx G_{m}(S)/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{2}(S)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{0,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{0,1}}

Figure 1.
The
E_{2}
-page of the 2-local Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence associated to the
C_{2}
-cover
{Y}(2)\to{Y}_{0}(2)
.

Remark 6.2
.

The values indicated in Figure
1
can be justified by a combination of Lemmas
3.22
and
3.21
.

•

For the bottom row (
j=0
), Lemma
3.22
shows that
H^{0}(C_{2},\mathbx G_{m}({Y}(2)_{S}))\cong\mathbx G_{m}(S)\oplus\mathbx Z
and that
H^{i}(C_{2},\mathbx G_{m}({Y}(2)_{S}))\cong H^{i}(C_{2},\mathbx G_{m}(S))
for
i\geq 1
. Since
C_{2}
acts trivially on
S
(and so also on
\mathbx G_{m}(S)
), the claimed values for
E_{2}^{i0}
(
i\geq 1
) now follow from Lemma
3.21
.

•

For the middle row (
j=1
), Lemma
3.22
shows that
H^{i}(C_{2},H^{1}({Y}(2)_{S},\mathbx G_{m}))\cong H^{i}(C_{2},\Pic(S))\oplus H^{i}(C_{2},\mu_{2}(S))
. As before,
C_{2}
acts trivially on
\Pic(S),\mu_{2}(S)
, so the claimed values for
E_{2}^{i,1}
now follow from Lemma
3.21
.

•

For the top row (
j=2
), we have
E_{2}^{0,2}=H^{0}(C_{2},H^{2}({Y}(2)_{S},\mathbx G_{m}))_{(2)}=H^{2}({Y}(2)_{S},\mathbx G_{m})^{C_{2}}\otimes\mathbx Z_{(2)}
by definition. Since
S
is regular and noetherian and
{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}\to S
is smooth,
{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}
is a regular, noetherian DM stack, so
[
AM20
, Proposition 2.5(iii)]
shows that
H^{2}({Y}(2)_{S},\mathbx G_{m})=\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})
. Hence,
E_{2}^{0,2}=\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\otimes\mathbx Z_{(2)}\cong\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}
.

The only object in Figure
1
which we would like to better understand is
E_{2}^{02}=\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}
. In order to compute this, we will take
C_{2}
-invariants of the exact sequence
[
AM20
, (5.6)]
(which ultimately comes from
[
AM20
, Proposition 3.2]
).

Proposition 6.3
.

There is an exact sequence

(13)

0\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(S)\oplus\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\longrightarrow H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})\oplus\mathbx Z/2\xrightarrow{\partial}\Br^{\prime}(S)[2].

Proof.

From
[
AM20
, Lemmas 5.10 and 5.11]
, we have a
C_{2}
-equivariant short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(S)\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(X_{S})\longrightarrow\widetilde{\rho}|_{C_{2}}\otimes H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q_{2}/\mathbx Z_{2})\longrightarrow 0,

where
X=\mathbx A^{1}\setminus\{0,1\}
and
\widetilde{\rho}
is the so-named representation appearing in Section
3.3
. This sequence is
C_{2}
-equivariantly split by pullback along the
C_{2}
-invariant section
-1\in X(S)
(recall that
C_{2}\curvearrowright X(S)
via
t\mapsto 1/t
). Thus,

(14)

H^{i}(C_{2},\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(X_{S}))\cong H^{i}(C_{2},\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(S))\oplus H^{i}\!\left(C_{2},\widetilde{\rho}|_{C_{2}}\otimes H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q_{2}/\mathbx Z_{2})\right)

for all
i\geq 0
. Now,
[
AM20
, (5.6)]
produces an exact sequence
0\to\Br^{\prime}(X_{S})\to\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})\to H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})\to 0
. Localizing at
2
and taking
C_{2}
-invariants (applying (
14
) and/or Lemma
3.21
where relevant), we obtain the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(S)\oplus H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q_{2}/\mathbx Z_{2})\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\longrightarrow H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})^{C_{2}}\xlongrightarrow{\partial}\Br^{\prime}(S)[2].

By Lemma
3.23
, we have
H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})^{C_{2}}\cong H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
, where the
\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
is generated by the image of
t/(t-1)^{2}
under the Kummer map
\mathbx G_{m}(X_{S})\to H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})
.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

In the exact sequence (
13
), the boundary map
\partial:H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\to\Br^{\prime}(S)[2]
is computed as follows. On the first factor, it sends
u\in H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})
to the class of the quaternion algebra
[(-1,u)]\in\Br^{\prime}(S)
. On the second factor, it sends the generator of
\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
to the class of the quaternion algebra
[(-1,-1)]\in\Br^{\prime}(S)
.

Proof.

By Lemma 5.13 of
[
AM20
]
, for
u\in H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})
, we have that
\partial(u)
equals the Brauer class of the cyclic quaternion algebra
(-1,u)
. We can use the same method in the proof of that lemma to compute
\partial
applied to the
\mathbx Z/2
term. Indeed, the proof there shows that the boundary map

\partial:H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})^{C_{2}}\rightarrow\Br^{\prime}(S)[2]

can be computed as follows. Let
X_{S}\xrightarrow{\pi}{Y}(2)_{S}\xrightarrow{c}X_{S}
be the projection composed with the coarse space map. Let
\chi\in H^{1}({Y}(2)_{S},C_{2})
be the class of the cocycle classifying
\pi
. Define
s\colon H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})\rightarrow\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})
by

s(u)\coloneqq[(\chi,u)]=[(\chi,c^{*}u)].

Given
u\in H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})
and
g\in C_{2}
,
\delta(u)\in H^{1}(C_{2},\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(X_{S}))
is given by the crossed homomorphism

(15)

g\mapsto\pi^{*}(g(s(u))-s(u))\in\Br^{\prime}(X_{S}).

Let
z:S\to X_{S}
denote the section
t=-1
.
The element in (
15
) can be considered an element of
\Br^{\prime}(S)[2]
via the isomorphisms

H^{1}(C_{2},\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(X_{S}))\xrightarrow{z^{*}}H^{1}(C_{2},\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(S))\xrightarrow{(23)}\Br^{\prime}(S)[2],

where the last map is evaluation of the crossed homomorphism at the nontrivial element
(23)\in C_{2}
. We remark
C_{2}
acts trivially on
u
since
u\in H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})^{C_{2}}

Thus, we have that
\partial\colon H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})^{C_{2}}\rightarrow\Br(S)[2]
is given by
z^{*}\pi^{*}((23)(s(u))-s(u))
.

For
u\in H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})\subset H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})^{C_{2}}
,
[
AM20
, Lemma 5.13]
show this gives
(-1,u)
.

Now we consider the image of
\delta
on the
\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
factor, namely on the
u
given by the image of
\langle\frac{t}{(t-1)^{2}}\rangle\in\mathbx G_{m}(X_{S})
in
H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})^{C_{2}}
. Here it is given by the pullback
z^{*}\pi^{*}(23)[(\chi,c^{*}u)]
. This is equal to
(-1,z^{*}\pi^{*}c^{*}u)
.

S\xrightarrow{z}X_{S}\xrightarrow{\pi}{Y}(2)_{S}\xrightarrow{c}X_{S}.

By going in the other direction in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx G_{m}(X_{S})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx G_{m}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(X_{S},\mu_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})}}

and the fact that
u
is obtained through the image of
\langle\frac{t}{(t-1)^{2}}\rangle\in\mathbx G_{m}(X_{S})
, we see that
z^{*}\pi^{*}c^{*}u
is the image of
-\frac{1}{4}\in\mathbx G_{m}(S)
(by plugging in
t=-1
in
\frac{t}{(t-1)^{2}})
in
H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})
. Thus the generator of
\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
is sent to the class of
(-1,-\frac{1}{4})=(-1,-1)
as desired.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

The exact sequence

(16)

0\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(S)\oplus\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\longrightarrow\ker\partial\longrightarrow 0

obtained from (
13
) is split.

Proof.

We will construct an explicit splitting map
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2}\colon\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\to\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(S)\oplus\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)
. We first consider the point
f\colon S\to{Y}(2)
given by the Legendre curve
E\colon y^{2}=x^{3}-x
(with discriminant
32\in\Gamma(S,O_{S})^{\times}
) and then set
\varphi_{1}=f^{*}\colon\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\to\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(S)
. Since
f
is a section of the structure map
{Y}(2)_{S}\to S
, its pullback map
\varphi_{1}
is a section to
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}
. Next, we define
\varphi_{2}\colon\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\to\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)=H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q_{2}/\mathbx Z_{2})
as the composition

\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\xlongrightarrow{\pi^{*}}\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(X_{S})^{C_{2}}\xrightarrow{\ram_{0}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q_{2}/\mathbx Z_{2}),

where the first arrow is pullback along the Legendre family
\pi:X_{S}\to{Y}(2)_{S}
and where
\ram_{0}
above computes the ramification along the
0
divisor
S\hookrightarrow\mathbx A^{1}_{S}\supset X_{S}
, as in
[
AM20
, Proposition 2.14 or Lemmas 5.10 and 5.11]
. We claim that
\varphi_{2}
is a section to the injection
\iota\colon\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\hookrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}
. From the proof of Proposition
6.3
, we see that this injection is computed as follows.

(
\star
)

Given
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)
, one lets
\beta\in\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(X_{S})
be the unique
C_{2}
-invariant Brauer class whose ramification along each of
0,1
is equal to
\alpha
and whose pullback along
-1\in X(S)^{C_{2}}
is trivial; then, one lets
\iota(\alpha)\in\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}
be the pullback of
\beta
along the coarse space map
c:{Y}(2)_{S}\to X_{S}
.

From this description, one now sees that
\varphi_{2}
is a section to
\iota
, as claimed. Indeed, given
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)
, if
\beta\in\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(X_{S})^{C_{2}}
is as in the (
\star
), then
\varphi_{2}(\iota(\alpha))=\ram_{0}(\pi^{*}c^{*}\beta)=\ram_{0}(\beta)=\alpha
because
c\circ\pi=\operatorname{id}_{X_{S}}
and because
\beta
was chosen to have ramification
\alpha
along
0
.
∎

Notation 6.6
.

•

We let
G^{\prime}=G^{\prime}(S):=\ker\partial
, with
\partial
the so-named map appearing in Proposition
6.3
. By Proposition
6.4
, we can describe
G^{\prime}
as the subgroup
G^{\prime}\subset H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})\oplus\{\pm 1\}
of pairs
(u,\pm 1)
such that
[(-1,u)]=[(-1,\pm 1)]\in\Br^{\prime}(S)
.

•

We let
G=G(S)
denote the subgroup
G\subset H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})
consisting of
u
’s such that
[(-1,u)]=0\in\Br^{\prime}(S)
as in
[
AM20
, Section 9]
.

We remark that
G^{\prime}
(resp.
G
) above are functorial; a morphism
S\to T
of (connected, regular, noetherian)
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-schemes induces a morphism
G^{\prime}(T)\to G^{\prime}(S)
(resp.
G(T)\to G(S)
).

Remark 6.7
.

The natural inclusion
G\hookrightarrow G^{\prime}
,
u\mapsto(u,+1)
induces a split exact sequence

0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow G^{\prime}\longrightarrow\{\pm 1\}\longrightarrow 0,

with splitting map
\{\pm 1\}\to G^{\prime}
sending
\pm 1\mapsto(\kappa_{2}(\pm 1),\pm 1)
, where
\kappa_{2}:\mathbx G_{m}(S)\to H^{1}(S,\mu_{2})
is the Kummer map.

6.2.
Determining the Differentials of the Spectral Sequence

We wish to compute the differentials on the
E_{2}
-page of the 2-local Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence associated to the
C_{2}
-cover
{Y}(2)\to{Y}_{0}(2)
. Recall that this is the spectral sequence

(17)

E_{2}^{i,j}=E_{2}^{i,j}(S)=H^{i}(C_{2},H^{j}({Y}(2)_{S},\mathbx G_{m}))_{(2)}\implies H^{i+j}({Y}_{0}(2)_{S},\mathbx G_{m})_{(2)}.

pictured in Figure
1
. We write

d_{r}^{i,j}=d_{r}^{i,j}(S):E_{r}^{i,j}(S)\longrightarrow E_{r}^{i+r,j+(r-1)}(S)

for the differential on the
r
th page, emphasizing the base scheme
S
if need be. By comparing this spectral sequence with the analogous spectral sequence

{}^{\prime}E_{2}^{i,j}=H^{i}(S_{3},H^{j}({Y}(2)_{S},\mathbx G_{m}))_{(2)}\implies H^{i+j}({M}_{1,1,S},\mathbx G_{m})_{(2)}

(with differentials denoted
{}^{\prime}d_{r}^{i,j}
) coming from the
S_{3}
-cover
{Y}(2)_{S}\to{M}_{1,1,S}
(see
[
AM20
, Section 9]
), we may understand most of the differentials
d_{r}^{i,j}
in our spectral sequence by leveraging the computations of the differentials
{}^{\prime}d_{r}^{i,j}
in
[
AM20
, Section 9]
. Indeed, the natural transformation
(-)^{S_{3}}\to(-)^{C_{2}}
of functors induces a morphism
({}^{\prime}E,{}^{\prime}d)\to(E,d)
of spectral sequences.

Lemma 6.8
.

Recall that
S
denotes a connected, regular noetherian
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-scheme. The differential
d_{2}^{0,1}
in the spectral sequence (
17
) vanishes. The differential
d_{2}^{1,1}
is always surjective, and is furthermore an isomorphism if
\Pic(S)[2]=0
.

Proof.

By comparing Figure
1
with
[
AM20
, Figure 3]
, we see that the morphism
({}^{\prime}E,{}^{\prime}d)\to(E,d)
of spectral sequences induces commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\Pic(S)_{(2)}\oplus\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\mathbx G_{m}(S)/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\Pic(S)_{(2)}\oplus\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\mathbx G_{m}(S)/2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\prime}d_{2}^{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{0,1}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\Pic(S)[2]\oplus\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\Pic(S)[2]\oplus\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\mu_{2}(S).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\prime}d_{2}^{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{1,1}}

The result follows from
[
AM20
, Proposition 9.2 and Corollary 9.5]
.
∎

Corollary 6.9
.

The differential
d_{3}^{0,2}
vanishes, so
E_{3}^{0,2}=E_{\infty}^{0,2}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.8
, the differential
d_{2}^{1,1}
is surjective, so
E_{3}^{3,0}=0
. Thus,
d_{3}^{0,2}:E_{3}^{0,2}\to E_{3}^{3,0}
must be the zero map. This is the last possibly nonzero differential the
(0,2)
object participates in, so we conclude that
E_{3}^{0,2}=E_{\infty}^{0,2}
.
∎

To compute the differential
d_{2}^{0,2}
, we use that the spectral sequence (
17
) is functorial in
S
.

We will also use Theorem
7.12
, which implies that
\Br^{\prime}{Y}_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}}\cong\mathbx Z/2
where
\overline{k}
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic not 2.

Lemma 6.10
.

If
\Pic(S)/2=0
, the differential

d_{2}^{0,2}\colon\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\longrightarrow\Pic(S)/2\oplus\mu_{2}(S)=\mu_{2}(S)

vanishes.

Proof.

We can pull back the whole sequence along a geometric point
\operatorname{Spec}\overline{k}\to S
in order to get a commutative square

\hbox to273.78pt{\vbox to57.86pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 136.8904pt\lower-28.09282pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
