On the
E_{2}
-page of the 2-local spectral sequence for
{Y}_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}}
, we are taking
S=\overline{k}
and the
(1,1)
-term is killed by the differential and the
(2,0)
term vanishes. Thus the morphism
\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}})\rightarrow E_{\infty}^{0,2}
is an isomorphism.

Using
S=\overline{k}
in the exact sequence of Proposition
6.3
shows that
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br(Y(2)_{\overline{k}})^{C_{2}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
, so the above square looks like

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br(Y(2))^{C_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\mu_{2}(\overline{k}),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{0,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

and we’re interested in understand the map
\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\to\mu_{2}(\overline{k})
.

By Theorem
7.12
, since
\ch\overline{k}\neq 2
we have
\Br(Y_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}})\cong\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
. Then the 2-local spectral sequence for
{Y}_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}}
implies that the bottom map
\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\rightarrow\mu_{2}(\overline{k})
must be zero, or otherwise the only nonzero term in the spectral sequence would be killed after the second page, in which case
\Br(Y_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}})=0
, contradiction. Then by the commutativity of the square above, this implies
d_{2}^{0,2}=0
as desired.
∎

In conclusion, if
\Pic(S)=0
then the
E_{2}
-page of the 2-local Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence we are considering is as indicated in Figure
2
(see Proposition
6.5
for the
E_{2}^{0,2}
-term).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}(S)\oplus\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q/Z)\oplus G^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx G_{m}(S)_{(2)}\oplus\mathbx Z_{(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{2}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx G_{m}(S)/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{2}(S)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Figure 2.
The
E_{2}
-page of the 2-local Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence associated to the
C_{2}
-cover
{Y}(2)_{S}\to{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}
, if
\Pic(S)=0
.

6.3.
Computing the 2-Primary Torsion

Assumption 6.11
.

Assume, for the rest of the section, that
\Pic(S)=0
.

By our computation of the spectral sequence (
17
) in the previous section, the terms on the second diagonal on the
E_{\infty}
-page are

\displaystyle E_{\infty}^{0,2}=\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br^{\prime}({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}

\displaystyle E_{\infty}^{1,1}=0

\displaystyle E_{\infty}^{2,0}=\mathbx G_{m}(S)/2.

Thus we have a short exact sequence

(18)

0\rightarrow\mathbx G_{m}(S)/2\rightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\rightarrow 0.

[
AM20
, Theorem 9.1]
produces an analogous short exact sequence for
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br(({M}_{1,1})_{S})
. The covering map
{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}\rightarrow({M}_{1,1})_{S}
induces the following map of short exact sequences,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx G_{m}(S)/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br(({M}_{1,1})_{S})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{S_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx G_{m}(S)/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

The snake lemma identifies the cokernels of the two rightmost vertical arrows, so we obtain

(19)

0\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br(({M}_{1,1})_{S})\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})\longrightarrow\coker[\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{S_{3}}\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}]\longrightarrow 0.

Lemma 6.12
.

The short exact sequence (
19
) is split.

Proof.

Recall that the morphism
f\colon{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}\rightarrow({M}_{1,1})_{S}
is a degree 3 finite cover. Thus we can define a norm map
f_{*}\mathbx G_{m}\rightarrow\mathbx G_{m}
by defining it to be the determinant function over any affine open on
({M}_{1,1})_{S}
for which
f_{*}\mathbx G_{m}
is finite and locally free. Precomposing with the natural map
\mathbx G_{m}\rightarrow f_{*}\mathbx G_{m}
, we see that a function
g\in\mathbx G_{m}(({M}_{1,1})_{S})
is sent to
g^{3}
. Thus the norm map induces a morphism

H^{2}(({Y}_{0}(2))_{S},\mathbx G_{m})\cong H^{2}(({M}_{1,1})_{S},f_{*}\mathbx G_{m})\rightarrow H^{2}(({M}_{1,1})_{S},\mathbx G_{m})

such that precomposition with the pullback
H^{2}(({M}_{1,1})_{S},\mathbx G_{m})\rightarrow H^{2}(({Y}_{0}(2))_{S},\mathbx G_{m})
is multiplication by 3 on
H^{2}(({M}_{1,1})_{S},\mathbx G_{m})
. Therefore, the 2-torsion exact sequence of Brauer groups is split as desired.
∎

It remains to compute
\coker[\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{S_{3}}\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}]
. We recall that the split exact sequence of Proposition
6.5
along with Remark
6.7
and the analogous split exact sequence for
({M}_{1,1})_{S}
, given in
[
AM20
, Proposition 5.14]
give compatible isomorphisms
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{S_{3}}\cong\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br(S)\oplus G
and
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}\cong\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br(S)\oplus\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\oplus G\oplus\mathbx Z/2
. Thus we have

(20)

\coker[\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{S_{3}}\longrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{S})^{C_{2}}]\cong\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(S,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\oplus\mathbx Z/2.

Thus we can compute the 2-torsion of
\Br_{{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}}
in terms of the 2-torsion of
\Br_{({M}_{1,1})_{S}}
. We recall the latter computation.

Proposition 6.13

(Proposition 9.7 of
[
AM20
]
)
.

Let
P
be a set of prime numbers including
2
and denote by
\mathbx Z_{P}\subset\mathbx Q
the
subset of all fractions where the denominator is only divisible by primes in
P
. Then

\Br(({M}_{1,1})_{\mathbx Z_{P}})\cong\Br(\mathbx Z_{P})~~\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in P\cup\{-1\},\\
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\mathbx Z/2~~\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in P,\\
p\not\equiv 3\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\mathbx Z/4.

Combining this result with Lemma
6.12
and (
20
), we arrive at the following conclusion.

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
P
be a set of prime numbers including
2
and denote by
\mathbx Z_{P}\subset\mathbx Q
the
subset of all fractions where the denominator is only divisible by primes in
P
. Then

\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{\mathbx Z_{P}})\cong\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(\mathbx Z_{P},\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\oplus\mathbx Z/2\oplus\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br(\mathbx Z_{P})~~\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in P\cup\{-1\},\\
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\mathbx Z/2~~\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in P,\\
p\not\equiv 3\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\mathbx Z/4.

Finally, we make the above proposition more explicit in the case that
\mathbx Z_{P}=\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
.

Lemma 6.15
.

H^{1}\!\left(\mathbx Z\!\left[\frac{1}{2}\right],\frac{\mathbx Q}{\mathbx Z}\right)\simeq\frac{\mathbx Z}{2\mathbx Z}\oplus\frac{\mathbx Q_{2}}{\mathbx Z_{2}}.

Proof.

We follow a similar strategy to the one employed in Lemma
4.5
.
Because cohomology commutes with colimits, we have

H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}],\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)=\varinjlim\limits_{n}H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}],\mathbx Z/n\mathbx Z).

We will show that
H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}],\mathbx Z/n\mathbx Z)=0
if
n
is odd, while
H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}],\mathbx Z/2^{k}\mathbx Z)\cong\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus\mathbx Z/2^{k}\mathbx Z
if
k\geq 1
. After showing this, taking the above direct limit will give the claim.

For
n
any positive integer,
[
Mil13
, Example 11.3]
gives the first isomorphism below:

H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}],\mathbx Z/n\mathbx Z)\simeq H^{1}_{\text{Group}}(\pi^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]),\mathbx Z/n\mathbx Z)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\pi^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]),\mathbx Z/n\mathbx Z).

At the same time,
\pi^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}])=:G
is the Galois group of the maximal extension
K/\mathbx Q
unramified away from
2
. By class field theory,
G^{\text{ab}}\simeq\mathbx Z^{\times}_{2}\cong\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus\mathbx Z_{2}
. Thus,

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\pi^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2p}]),\mathbx Z/n\mathbx Z)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus\mathbx Z_{2},\mathbx Z/n\mathbx Z).

Hence, if
n
is odd, then
H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}],\mathbx Z/n\mathbx Z)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus\mathbx Z_{2},\mathbx Z/n\mathbx Z)=0
. On the other hand, if
n=2^{k}
for some
k\geq 1
, then
H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}],\mathbx Z/2^{k}\mathbx Z)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus\mathbx Z_{2},\mathbx Z/2^{k}\mathbx Z)\simeq\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus\mathbx Z/2^{k}\mathbx Z
. The claim follows.
∎

Corollary 6.16
.

\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}_{0}(2))\cong\mathbx Q_{2}/\mathbx Z_{2}\oplus(\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z)^{\oplus 4}\oplus\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
6.14
to
P=\{2\}
, using Lemma
6.15
to compute
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H^{1}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H^{1}(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}],\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)
and
[
AM20
, Example 2.19(2)]
to compute
\Br(\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}])\cong\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
.
∎

7.
The Brauer Group of
{Y}_{0}(2)
over an Algebraically Closed Field

Following
[
Shi19
]
, we will compute the Brauer group of
{Y}_{0}(2)
over an algebraically closed field
k
whose characteristic is not 2.

7.1.
Preliminaries

Recall (Proposition
3.11
) that the coarse space of
{Y}_{0}(2)
is
Y_{0}(2)=\mathbx A^{1}\setminus\{0\}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}][s,s^{-1}]
. Our first task in the current section will be to show that, away from the point
s=-1/4
,
{Y}_{0}(2)
is a trivial
C_{2}
-gerbe over its coarse space.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\pi:{Y}_{0}(2)\to Y_{0}(2)
denote the coarse space map of Proposition
3.11
. Consider the family of elliptic curves

(21)

E:y^{2}=x\!\left(x^{2}-(8s+2)x+4s(1+4s)\right)

over
Y_{0}(2)\setminus\{-1/4\}
, equipped with two torsion point
P=(0,0)
. The pair
(E,P)
defines a section
Y_{0}(2)\setminus\{-1/4\}\to{Y}_{0}(2)
of
\pi
.

Proof.

One can compute that the family
E\to Y_{0}(2)\setminus\{-1/4\}
has discriminant
1024s^{2}(1+4s)^{3}
, which is an invertible function on
Y_{0}(2)\setminus\{-1/4\}
. Thus,
E
really defines a family of elliptic curves, and it is visibly clear that
P=(0,0)
is a nonzero 2-torsion section of this family, so
(E,P)
defines a map
f:Y_{0}(2)\setminus\{-1/4\}\to{Y}_{0}(2)
. It remains to show that this is a section of
\pi
, i.e. that its
s
-invariant is the inclusion
Y_{0}(2)\setminus\{-1/4\}\hookrightarrow Y_{0}(2)
. In order to compute its
s
-invariant, we first remark that over
Y_{0}(2)[\sqrt{1+4s}]:=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}][s,s^{-1},\sqrt{1+4s}]
, the family
E
becomes

y^{2}=x(x-\alpha)(x-\overline{\alpha})\,\text{ where }\,\alpha=1+4s-\sqrt{1+4s},

which, by Proposition
3.5
, gives a point of
Y(2)
with
t
-invariant

t\coloneqq\frac{\alpha-0}{\overline{\alpha}-0}=\frac{1+2s-\sqrt{1+4s}}{2s}.

Thus,
(E,P)
has
s
-invariant

\frac{t}{(t-1)^{2}}=s,

as desired.
∎

Notation 7.2
.

Let
Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}:=Y_{0}(2)\setminus\{-1/4\}
and
{Y}_{0}(2)^{\circ}:={Y}_{0}(2)\times_{Y_{0}(2)}Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}
. Let
\pi^{\circ}:{Y}_{0}(2)^{\circ}\to Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}
be the restriction of the coarse space map.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
S
be an arbitrary
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-scheme. Suppose that
(E,P),(E^{\prime},P^{\prime})\in{Y}_{0}(2)^{\circ}(S)
are two families of elliptic curves, each equipped with a point of order
2
, whose
s
-invariants agree, as morphisms
S\to Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}
. Then, there exists an étale cover
S^{\prime}\to S
supporting an isomorphism
(E_{S^{\prime}},P)\cong(E^{\prime}_{S^{\prime}},P^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
s\in H^{0}(S,O_{S})
be the coarse space map
S\to Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}\subset\mathbx A^{1}
induced by
(E,P)
. It suffices to consider a single
(E,P)\in{Y}_{0}(2)^{\circ}(S)
and show that, after passage to an étale cover, it becomes isomorphic to the family

E^{\prime}:y^{2}=x\!\left(x^{2}-(8s+2)x+4s(1+4s)\right)\,\text{ with }\,P^{\prime}=(0,0)

of Lemma
7.1
. For this, we may assume that
S=\operatorname{Spec}R
is affine. By
[
AM20
, Proof of Proposition 4.5]
, there exists an (affine) étale cover
\operatorname{Spec}R^{\prime}\to\operatorname{Spec}R
such that
E_{R^{\prime}}
can be put in Legendre form. Once it is in this form, we can perform a linear change of variables to move
P
to the origin. Thus, we may assume without loss of generality that, over
S=\operatorname{Spec}R
, we have:

E:y^{2}=x(x-1)(x-t)\,\text{ and }\,P=(0,0).

We seek an isomorphism
\varphi:E\to E^{\prime}
with
\varphi(0,0)=(0,0)
. We first remark that, by assumption, the quantities

\begin{matrix}\displaystyle t-1,&&\displaystyle s=\frac{t}{(t-1)^{2}},&&\displaystyle 4s+1\end{matrix}

are all units in
R=H^{0}(S,O_{S})
. Thus, we see that
(t+1)^{2}=(4s+1)(t-1)^{2}
is also a unit. Since
R
is a
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-algebra, this means that
2(t+1)\in R^{\times}
as well. Hence,
R^{\prime}:=R[\sqrt{2t+2}]
is an étale
R
-algebra. Now, over
R^{\prime}
, we can define our desired isomorphism
\varphi:E_{R^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\sim}E^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
via

\varphi(x,y):=\!\left(u^{2}x,u^{3}y\right)\,\text{ where }\,u:=\frac{\sqrt{2t+2}}{t-1}\in\!\left(R^{\prime}\right)^{\times}.\qed

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
S
be an arbitrary
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-scheme, and fix some
(E,P)\in{Y}_{0}(2)^{\circ}(S)
. Then,
\underline{\Aut}(E,P)\simeq\underline{C_{2}}
as sheaves on
S_{\text{\'{e}t}}
.

Proof.

Because
E
is of finite presentation over
S
, we may reduce to the case that
S
is noetherian. We may furthermore assume that
S
is connected and then prove that
\Aut(E/S,P)=\{\pm 1\}
. In fact,
[
Ols16
, 13.1.11]
shows that the automorphism scheme
\underline{\Aut}(E)
(so also the subscheme
\underline{\Aut}(E,P)\hookrightarrow\underline{\Aut}(E)
) is (formally) unramified over
S
. Because
S_{\mathrm{red}}\hookrightarrow S
is a finite order thickening (since
S
is noetherian), we deduce from this that the natural map
\Aut(E/S,P)\to\Aut(E/S_{\text{red}},P)
is injective, so we may assume that
S
is reduced. Because
S
is assumed connected, by passage to its irreducible components, we may assume that
S
is integral. That is, it suffices to prove that
\Aut(E/S,P)=\{\pm 1\}
whenever
S
is integral.

Because
P
is 2-torsion, it is clear that
\{\pm 1\}\subset\Aut(E/S,P)
. If
\Aut(E/S)=\{\pm 1\}
, we are done, so suppose this is not the case. Let
\eta\in\Aut(E/S)
be an automorphism fixing
P
. Then, at worse,
\eta
swaps the other two 2-torsion sections of
E
, so
\eta^{2}
acts trivially on
E[2]
. By
[
KM85
, Corollary 2.7.2]
, this forces
\eta^{2}=\pm 1
. Thus, either
\eta=\pm 1
, in which case we win, or
E/S
supports an automorphism of order
4
. Suppose the latter. Then,
E
must have constant
j
-invariant
1728\in\Gamma(S,O_{S})
. By Lemma
3.16
, the
s
-invariant of
(E,P)
then satisfies
256(s+1)^{3}/s^{2}=1728
. Since
\Gamma(S,O_{S})
is a domain by assumption, one can check that this forces
s=-1/4
or
s=2
; since
(E,P)
has
s
-invariant landing in
Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}=Y_{0}(2)\setminus\{-1/4\}
by assumption, we conclude that
(E,P)
must have constant
s
-invariant
2
and that
3\in\Gamma(S,O_{S})^{\times}
. Indeed, since
2=s\neq-1/4
,
8\neq-1
, so
9\neq 0
at every point on
S
, and thus 3 is a unit on
S
.
Now, Lemma
7.3
shows that families with the same
s
-invariant are always locally isomorphic. Combined with Lemma
7.1
, this shows that (after étale base change), we may assume that
E/S
is of the form

y^{2}=x(x^{2}-18x+72)=x(x-6)(x-12)

with
P=(0,0)
. Making the linear change of variables
x\rightsquigarrow x+6
shows that
E
is isomorphic to the curve

y^{2}=(x+6)x(x-6)=x^{3}-36x

with
P=(-6,0)
. Because
3
is invertible on
S
, one can check that the automorphism group of this curve is
\mu_{4}(S)
with any
\zeta\in\mu_{4}(S)
acting via
[\zeta](x,y)=\!\left(\zeta^{2}x,\zeta y\right)
. From this description, it is finally clear that
\Aut(E/S,P)=\{\pm 1\}
.
∎

Lemma 7.5

(Compare
[
Shi19
, Lemma 3.2]
)
.

The coarse space map
\pi^{\circ}:{Y}_{0}(2)^{\circ}\to Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}
is a trivial
C_{2}
-gerbe. Hence,
{Y}_{0}(2)^{\circ}\simeq BC_{2,Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.1
, the restriction
\pi^{\circ}:{Y}_{0}(2)^{\circ}\to Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}
of the coarse space map has a section. Thus, it suffices to show that
{Y}_{0}(2)^{\circ}
is a
C_{2}
-gerbe over
Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}
, i.e. that families with the same
s
-invariant are (étale-)locally isomorphic and each have automorphism sheaf
\underline{C_{2}}
. The first of these is Lemma
7.3
while the second is Lemma
7.4
.
∎

Our goal is now to compute the Brauer group of
{Y}_{0}(2)
over an algebraically closed fields by exploiting this gerbe structure along with knowledge of the coarse space.

To get started with our computation, we begin with the following immediate consequence of
[
Shi19
, Lemma 3.3]
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field with
\ch k\neq 2
. Then,
\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})
is a subgroup of
\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
.

Proof.

By
[
AM20
, Proposition 2.5(iv)]
, the restriction map
\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})\to\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k}^{\circ})
is an injection. Since
{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}^{\circ}\simeq BC_{2,Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}}
by Lemma
7.5
and
\Pic Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}_{k}=0
,
[
Shi19
, Lemma 3.3]
gives the second equality in

\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k}^{\circ})=\Br^{\prime}(BC_{2,Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}_{k}})=\mathbx G_{m}(Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}_{k})/2=\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z.

The last equality holds because
\mathbx G_{m}(Y_{0}(2)^{\circ}_{k})=(k[s,s^{-1},(s+1/4)^{-1}])^{\times}=k^{\times}\times sZ\times(s+1/4)Z.

∎

7.2.
The Computation

Setup 7.7
.

Fix an algebraically closed field
k
with
\Char k\neq 2
.

We compute
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
following the strategy of
[
Shi19
, Section 4]
. We first observe that
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
is a
2
-torsion group by Lemma
7.6
. For
n\geq 1
, we have
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}=H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mathbx G_{m})[2^{n}]
, so the Kummer sequence
0\to\mu_{2^{n}}\to\mathbx G_{m}\to\mathbx G_{m}\to 0
gives rise to the short exact sequence

(22)

0\longrightarrow\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}/2^{n}\longrightarrow H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\longrightarrow\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\longrightarrow 0.

Remark 7.8
.

For computing
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
, although (
22
) shows that it would suffice to compute
H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})
for
n=1
alone, we will instead find it easier to take a colimit of (
22
) as
n
varies, computing the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim\limits_{n}\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}/2^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim\limits_{n}H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z.}

in the proof of Theorem
7.12
. In fact, Remark
7.9
and Theorem
7.12
will show that the two colimits above stabilize already at the
n=2
terms.

Remark 7.9
.

By
[
Nil16
, Theorem 1.1]
(or Theorem
A.3
if
\Char k=3
),
\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}=\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
if
\Char k\neq 2
.

In light of the above, we are interested in understanding
H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})
. To ease notation, set
U:=Y_{0}(2)_{k}^{\circ}
. Following
[
Shi19
]
, let
Z\hookrightarrow Y_{0}(2)_{k}=\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\}
denote the complement of
U
, with its induced reduced structure (so
Z\simeq\operatorname{Spec}k
). With this notation setup, we have the following commutative diagram of Cartesian squares whose vertical morphisms are coarse space maps,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{Y}_{0}(2)^{\circ}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{Y}_{0}(2)_{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}(2)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

As in
[
Shi19
, (4.1.4)]
, the distinguished triangle

j_{!}j^{*}R\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}}\rightarrow R\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}}\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}R\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}}\xrightarrow{+1}

combined with the fact that
j^{*}R\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}}\cong R\pi^{\circ}_{*}\mu_{2^{n}}
gives the following long exact sequence.

(23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\longrightarrow H^{0}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{2^{n}})\longrightarrow H^{0}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\longrightarrow H^{0}(Z,i^{*}R\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}})\\
&\longrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{2^{n}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(Z,i^{*}R\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}})\\
&\longrightarrow H^{2}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{2^{n}})\longrightarrow H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\longrightarrow H^{2}(Z,i^{*}R\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}})\\
&\longrightarrow\cdots\end{split}

To compute the terms
H^{s}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{2^{n}})
, we will use the following result, in which
j
is allowed to be slightly more general.

Lemma 7.10
.

Fix an integer
r\geq 1
. Let
j\colon U\hookrightarrow\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
be the inclusion of the complement of
r
distinct
k
-points, and let
\ell
be a positive integer relatively prime to the characteristic of
k
. Then, we have a Galois-equivariant exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mu_{\ell}(k)^{r-1}\longrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},j_{!}\mu_{\ell})\longrightarrow\mathbx Z/\ell\mathbx Z\longrightarrow 0,

while
H^{s}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},j_{!}\mu_{\ell})=0
for
s\neq 1
. In particular,
H^{1}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},j_{!}\mu_{\ell})\cong\mu_{\ell}(k)^{r-1}\oplus\mathbx Z/\ell\mathbx Z
as abelian groups.

Proof.

Let
i\colon Z\rightarrow\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
be the complement of
j\colon U\rightarrow\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
, with its reduced induced scheme structure. The long exact sequence associated to
0\rightarrow j_{!}j^{*}\mu_{\ell}\rightarrow\mu_{\ell}\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}\mu_{\ell}\rightarrow 0
gives

0\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}\mu_{\ell})\rightarrow\mu_{\ell}(k)\rightarrow\mu_{\ell}(k)^{\oplus r}\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}\mu_{\ell})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},\mu_{\ell})\rightarrow 0.

The last term is 0 because the dimension of
Z
is 0.
The morphism
\mu_{\ell}(k)\rightarrow\mu_{\ell}(k)^{\oplus r}
is the diagonal, so we have
H^{0}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}\mu_{\ell})=0
. Using the Kummer sequence, we have
H^{1}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},\mu_{\ell})=\mathbx G_{m}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\})/\ell=\mathbx Z/\ell\mathbx Z
. This gives the claimed exact sequence for
H^{1}
(which is automatically split as a sequence of
\mathbx F_{\ell}
-vector spaces). In higher degrees,
H^{s}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}\mu_{\ell})=0

for
s\geq 2
, because affine etale cohomological dimension is the dimension of the scheme.
∎

We can now compute the terms in the leftmost column of our long exact sequence (
23
).

Lemma 7.11

(Compare
[
Shi19
, Lemma 4.3]
)
.

Let
m
be any positive integer. In the setup of Lemma
7.10
, let
\pi^{\circ}\colon BC_{m,U}\to U
be the trivial
C_{m}
-gerbe over
U
. We have

H^{n}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{\ell})\cong\begin{cases}0&n=0,\\
\mu_{\ell}^{\oplus r-1}\oplus\mathbx Z/\ell&n=1,\\
\mu_{\gcd(m,\ell)}^{\oplus r-1}\oplus\mathbx Z/\gcd(m,\ell)&n\geq 2,\end{cases}

where the above isomorphisms are not necessarily Galois equivariant.

Proof.

Given an object
\mathcal{C}
in the derived category of sheaves on some scheme, let
h^{t}(\mathcal{C})
denote its
t
th cohomology sheaf. Since the functor
j_{!}
is exact, we observe that
h^{t}(j_{!}R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{\ell})\simeq j_{!}h^{t}(R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{\ell})=j_{!}R^{t}\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{\ell}
. Thus, the hypercohomology spectral sequence
[
Wei94
, 5.7.9]

E_{2}^{st}=H^{s}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},h^{t}(\mathcal{C}))\implies H^{s+t}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k},\mathcal{C})

applied to
\mathcal{C}=j_{!}R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{\ell}
becomes

E^{st}_{2}=H^{s}\!\left(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},j_{!}R^{t}\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{\ell}\right)\implies H^{s+t}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},j_{!}R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{\ell}).

We claim that the
E_{2}
-page of this sequence has only one nonzero column, the
s=1
column. Indeed,
[
Shi19
, Lemma B.1]
shows that

R^{t}\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{\ell}\simeq\begin{cases}\hfill\displaystyle\mu_{\ell}\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle t=0\\
\hfill\displaystyle\mu_{\ell}[m]\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle t>0\text{ odd}\\
\hfill\displaystyle\mu_{\ell}/m\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle t>0\text{ even}\end{cases}.

Combined with Lemma
7.10
and the observations that
\mu_{\ell}[m]=\mu_{\gcd(m,\ell)}\cong\mu_{\ell}/m
, we conclude that
E^{st}_{2}=0
if
s\neq 1
while

\displaystyle E^{1,t-1}_{2}=H^{1}\!\left(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},j_{!}R^{t-1}\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}\hfill\displaystyle H^{1}\!\left(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},j_{!}\mu_{\ell}\right)\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle t=1\\
\hfill\displaystyle H^{1}\!\left(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},j_{!}(\mu_{\ell}[m])\right)\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle t>1\text{ even}\\
\hfill\displaystyle H^{1}\!\left(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},j_{!}(\mu_{\ell}/m)\right)\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle t>1\text{ odd}\end{cases}

(24)

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}\hfill\displaystyle\mu_{\ell}(k)^{\oplus(r-1)}\oplus\mathbx Z/\ell\mathbx Z\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle t=1\\
\hfill\displaystyle\mu_{\gcd(m,\ell)}^{\oplus(r-1)}(k)\oplus\mathbx Z/\gcd(m,\ell)\mathbx Z\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle t>1.\end{cases}

Since this
E_{2}
-page is a concentrated in a single column, we must have
H^{n}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\},j_{!}R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{\ell})\simeq E_{2}^{1,n-1}
, from which the claim follows.
∎

Taking
m=2
,
r=1
, and
\ell=2^{n}
in the above lemma, we obtain the terms in the left column of the long exact sequence (
23
). Next we wish to compute the terms in the right column, namely the groups
H^{s}(Z,i^{*}R\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}})
. Recall that in our situation,
i\colon Z\rightarrow\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
is the inclusion of the point
-\frac{1}{4}
(so
Z\simeq\operatorname{Spec}k
). Consider the stacky point of
{Y}_{0}(2)
that gets mapped to
-\frac{1}{4}
. It comes from the elliptic curve corresponding to
t=-1
in the Legendre family, i.e. to
E:y^{2}=x(x-1)(x+1)=x^{3}-x
equipped with the order two point
P=(0,0)
. If
\ch(k)>3
, then this curve has automorphism group
\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
generated by
(x,y)\mapsto(-x,iy)
; if
\ch(k)=3
, then it has an automorphism group of order
12
generated by the previous automorphism along with the order 3 automorphism
(x,y)\mapsto(x+1,y)
. Thus, in any case, the group of automorphisms preserving
P
is simply
\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
.

As in
[
Shi19
, Section 4.4]
, for any
n\geq 0
, we have the following sequence of isomorphisms, explained below.

(25)

H^{s}(Z,i^{*}R\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}})\overset{(a)}{\cong}i^{*}R^{s}\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}}\overset{(b)}{\cong}H^{s}({Y}_{0}(2)_{Z},\mu_{2^{n}})\overset{(c)}{\cong}H^{s}((B\mathbx Z/4)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\overset{(d)}{\cong}H^{s}(\mathbx Z/4,\mu_{2^{n}}(k)).

Above,
(a)
holds because
Z\cong\operatorname{Spec}k
;
(b)
holds by proper base change
[
Ols05
, Theorem 1.3]
;
(c)
holds because
({Y}_{0}(2)_{Z})_{\mathrm{red}}\cong(B\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z)_{k}
and so one can apply topological invariance of étale cohomology
[
Sta23
,
Tag 03SI
]
(note that
\mu_{2^{n},{Y}_{0}(2)_{Z}}|_{B\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z}\simeq\mu_{2^{n},B\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z}
as étale sheaves because
\mu_{2^{n}}
is étale over
{Y}_{0}(2)_{Z}
); and
(d)
follows from the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence applied to the
\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
-cover
\operatorname{Spec}k\to(B\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z)_{k}
. In (
25
),
\mathbx Z/4
acts trivially on
\mu_{2^{n}}(k)
, so

(26)

H^{s}(Z,i^{*}R\pi_{*}\mu_{2^{n}})\cong H^{s}(\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z,\mu_{2^{n}}(k))\cong\begin{cases}\hfill\displaystyle\mu_{2^{n}}(k)\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle s=0\\
\hfill\displaystyle\mu_{2^{n}}(k)[4]\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle s>0\text{ odd}\\
\hfill\displaystyle\mu_{2^{n}}(k)/4\hfill&\text{if }\displaystyle s>0\text{ even}\end{cases}

by Fact
3.20
.

The final ingredient we will use is the computation of the terms in the middle column of (
23
) in degrees 0 and 1. First, we have
H^{0}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\cong H^{0}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},\mu_{2^{n}})\cong\mu_{2^{n}}
. By Proposition 2.9 of
[
AM20
]
, we have a split short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathbx G_{m}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})/\mathbx G_{m}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})^{2^{n}}\rightarrow H^{1}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\rightarrow\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})[2^{n}]\rightarrow 0.

Because
k
is algebraically closed, using Proposition
3.11
, we have
\mathbx G_{m}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})=\mathbx G_{m}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\})=sZ
, where
\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\}=\operatorname{Spec}k[s,s^{-1}]
. By Remark
7.9
,
\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})\cong\mathbx Z/(4)
. Thus, for
n\geq 2
, this gives an isomorphism
H^{1}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\cong\mathbx Z/(2^{n})\oplus\mathbx Z/(4)
.

Thus for
n\geq 2
, the long exact sequence (
23
) becomes the following.

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow\mu_{2^{n}}(k)\longrightarrow\mu_{2^{n}}(k)\\
&\longrightarrow\mathbx Z/2^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbx Z/2^{n}\oplus\mathbx Z/4\longrightarrow\mu_{4}(k)\\
&\longrightarrow\mathbx Z/2\longrightarrow H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\longrightarrow\mu_{2^{n}}(k)/4\\
&\longrightarrow\cdots\end{split}

Looking at these terms, we conclude that we must have an exact sequence of the form

0\rightarrow\mathbx Z/2\rightarrow H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\rightarrow\mu_{2^{n}}(k)/4.

We will need to understand how this sequence behaves functorially with
n
. We recall that the
\mathbx Z/2
here is computed in (
7.2
) in the proof of Lemma
7.11
as

H^{2}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}R\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{2^{n}})\cong H^{1}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}R^{1}\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{2^{n}})\cong H^{1}(\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\},j_{!}\mu_{2})\cong\mathbx Z/2.

where the penultimate congruence uses the fact that

R^{1}\pi_{*}^{\circ}\mu_{2^{n}}\cong\mu_{2^{n}}[2]\cong\mu_{2}.

This is significant because this implies that as
n
varies, the corresponding morphism between these cohomology groups is an isomorphism.
On the other hand, we see that as we go from
n
to
n+2
, the corresponding morphism
\mu_{2^{n}}/(4)\rightarrow\mu_{2^{n+2}}/(4)
is 0. These observations allow us to prove the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 7.12

(Compare Theorem 1.1 of
[
Shi19
]
)
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field with
\ch k\neq 2
. then
\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})\cong\mathbx Z/2
.

Proof.

By the discussion preceding this theorem statement, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx Z/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx Z/4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx Z/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n+2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbx Z/4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

with exact rows. Observe that the morphism
f_{n}
indicated above both lands in
\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\subset H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n+2}})
and restricts to the identity map on
\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
. Thus, it gives a splitting of the top left exact sequence, so

H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\cong\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus G,\,\text{ where }G:=\operatorname{im}\!\left(H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\to\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z\right).

Since
\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}=\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
, and so
\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}/2^{n}=\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
, the embedding
\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\hookrightarrow H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})
(see (
22
)) shows that
H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})
must contain an element of order
4
. Since
H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\cong\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus G
, we must have
G=\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
and so
H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2^{n}})\cong\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\oplus\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
. From (
22
), we conclude that
\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})=\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
.
∎

To finish this section, we would like to say a bit more about the generator of
\Br^{\prime}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})\cong\mathbx Z/2
. In particular, we will prove that it is not represented by a quaternion algebra over
{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
.

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
\alpha\in\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
denote the nontrivial element. Then,
\alpha
is a sum of classes of quaternion algebras. Equivalently, the image of

(27)

H^{1}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},C_{2})\otimes H^{1}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2})\xlongrightarrow{\smile}H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2})\longrightarrow H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mathbx G_{m})=\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}

is
0
.

Proof.

Let
f\colon{Y}(2)_{k}\to{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
denote the natural map, and recall from Proposition
3.5
that
{Y}(2)_{k}
has coarse space
c\colon{Y}(2)_{k}\to X_{k}=\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0,1\}
. The exact sequence (
18
) from Section
6
shows that
f^{*}\colon\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})\to\Br({Y}(2)_{k})
is injective. Thus, it suffices to show that any element in the image of (
27
) pulls back to
0\in\Br{Y}(2)_{k}
. Since
\underline{C_{2}}\simeq\mu_{2}
over
k
, it suffices to show that

(28)

\operatorname{im}\!\left(f^{*}\colon H^{1}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mu_{2})\to H^{1}({Y}(2)_{k},\mu_{2})\right)\subset\operatorname{im}\!\left(c^{*}\colon H^{1}(X_{k},\mu_{2})\to H^{1}({Y}(2)_{k},\mu_{2})\right),

since then any quaternion algebra over
{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
pulls back to an element of
\operatorname{im}\!\left(c^{*}\colon\Br X_{k}\to\Br{Y}(2)_{k}\right)=0
(note that
\Br X_{k}=0
by Tsen’s theorem).

With this in mind, consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbx G_{m}({Y}_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}})/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}({Y}_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}},\mu_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}})[2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbx G_{m}({Y}(2)_{\overline{k}})/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}({Y}(2)_{\overline{k}},\mu_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pic({Y}(2)_{\overline{k}})[2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbx G_{m}(X_{\overline{k}})/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X_{\overline{k}},\mu_{2}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{*}}

whose rows are exact. We claim that the map on
\Pic
’s is trivial; this suffices to show (
28
). By Theorem
A.3
,
\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}}\cong\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
. At the same time, Propositions
3.5
and
3.17
show that
\Pic{Y}(2)_{\overline{k}}\cong\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
. Thus,
f^{*}\colon\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}})\to\Pic({Y}(2)_{\overline{k}})
is a map
\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z\to\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
and so necessarily becomes trivial when restricted to
2
-torsion. This completes the proof.
∎

8.
\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})
over other fields
k

In this section, we wish to compute
\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})
for fairly general fields
k
. We first remark that using the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequences associated to the covers
X\to{Y}(2)\to{Y}_{0}(2)
, as we did when computing e.g.
\Br({Y}_{0}(2)Q)
, would present at least two challenges:

•

If
\Char k=p\geq 3
, our general result Proposition
4.2
for computing
p
-primary torsion would not apply.

•

In any case, computing
2
-primary torsion would require solving the extension problem presented by (
18
).

For these reasons, we adopt a new approach in this section. Since we will be working over a field
k
, we can consider the Galois cover
{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}\to{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
, where
k^{s}/k
is a separable closure of
k
, and apply the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence to it. Because we have computed
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{\overline{k}}
in Section
7
, this spectral sequence will allow us to compute
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
for many perfect fields
k
.

Remark 8.1
.

Despite the warning just given about attempting to do this computation using the covers
X\to{Y}(2)\to{Y}_{0}(2)
, the argument in the current section still relies non-trivially on the work in Section
6
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic not
2
. Then, there is an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\Br(k)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\longrightarrow\ker\!\left(\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\longrightarrow\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}\right)\longrightarrow H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z)\longrightarrow H^{3}(k,\mathbx G_{m})\oplus H^{3}(k,\mathbx Z).

Proof.

This follows from writing down the exact sequence of low degree terms (see e.g.
[
Poo17
, Proposition 6.7.1]
) associated to the spectral sequence

E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}(k,H^{q}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}},\mathbx G_{m}))\implies H^{p+q}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mathbx G_{m}).

In general, this exact sequence takes the form

0\to E_{2}^{1,0}\to H^{1}\to E_{2}^{0,1}\to E_{2}^{2,0}\to\ker\!\left(H^{2}\to E_{2}^{0,2}\right)\to E_{2}^{1,1}\to E_{2}^{3,0}.

In the present context, we compute the individual terms as follows.
Below, note that
H^{0}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}},\mathbx G_{m})=H^{0}(Y_{0}(2)_{k^{s}},\mathbx G_{m})=\mathbx G_{m}(k^{s})\oplus\mathbx Z
.

•

E_{2}^{1,0}=H^{1}(k,\mathbx G_{m}(k^{s})\oplus\mathbx Z)=\Pic k\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z)=0
.

•

H^{1}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mathbx G_{m})=\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\cong\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
by Theorem
A.3
.

•

E_{2}^{0,1}=H^{0}(k,H^{1}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}},\mathbx G_{m}))\cong\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
by Theorem
A.3
.

•

E_{2}^{2,0}=H^{2}(k,H^{0}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}},\mathbx G_{m}))=H^{2}(k,\mathbx G_{m}(k^{s})\oplus\mathbx Z)=\Br k\oplus H^{2}(k,\mathbx Z)\cong\Br k\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)
with the last isomorphism following from taking cohomology of the exact sequence
0\to\mathbx Z\to\mathbx Q\to\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z\to 0
.

•

H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k},\mathbx G_{m})=\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
by Lemma
2.5
.

•

E_{2}^{0,2}=H^{0}(k,H^{2}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}},\mathbx G_{m}))=H^{0}(k,\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}))
.

•

E^{1,1}_{2}=H^{1}(k,H^{1}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}},\mathbx G_{m}))=H^{1}(k,\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}})=H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z)
. Here, the Galois action on
\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}\cong\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
is trivial since Theorem
A.3
shows that
\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\to\Pic{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}
is an isomorphism (with both groups generated by the Hodge bundle).

•

E^{3,0}_{2}=H^{3}(k,H^{0}({Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}},\mathbx G_{m}))=H^{3}(k,\mathbx G_{m})\oplus H^{3}(k,\mathbx Z)
.

Note also that

\ker\!\left(\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\longrightarrow H^{0}(k,\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}})\right)=\ker\!\left(\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\longrightarrow\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}\right)

because
H^{0}(k,\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}})\subseteq\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}
.
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic not
2
. Then, the natural map

\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\longrightarrow\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}\cong\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z

is a split surjection.

Proof.

First note that
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}\cong\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}\cong\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
by Theorem
7.12
, since
k
is perfect. By the work in Section
6
(see in particular (
18
)), the map
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\to\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}
extends to the following homomorphism of short exact sequences:

(29)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbx G_{m}(k)/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}(\Br{Y}(2)_{k})^{C_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbx G_{m}(k^{s})/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k^{s}})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k^{s}})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k^{s}})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k^{s}})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}(\Br{Y}(2)_{k^{s}})^{C_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the above-indicated horizontal map is an isomorphism because
\Char k^{s}\neq 2
, so
\mathbx G_{m}(k^{s})/2=0
.

We claim that the map
f
indicated above is surjective. Using functoriality of exact sequence (
16
) along with Notation
6.6
and Remark
6.7
to compute
\ker\partial
, with obtain the following commutative diagram

(30)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br(k)\oplus\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{k})^{C_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(k)\oplus\{\pm 1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{k^{s}})^{C_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\pm 1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

with exact rows. Note that
f
is surjective if and only if the rightmost vertical map above is. Remark
6.7
, applied once to
S=\operatorname{Spec}k^{s}
and then to
S=\operatorname{Spec}k
, shows the the rightmost vertical map is a surjection, with kernel
G(k)
.

This is enough to conclude that
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\to\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}=\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k^{s}})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k^{s}})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k^{s}})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{(\Br{Y}(2)_{k^{s}})}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}(\Br{Y}(2)_{k^{s}})^{C_{2}}
is surjective. In order to show that it is a split surjection, we appeal to Lemma
6.12
, or rather, to its proof. The proof of this lemma (in particular, (
20
)) shows that
\coker\!\left[\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{k})^{S_{3}}\to\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{k})^{C_{2}}\right]\cong\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
, where this
\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
is, more canonically,
G^{\prime}(k)/G(k)
. The above discussion of the diagram (
30
) identifies
G^{\prime}(k)/G(k)\simeq\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{k^{s}})^{C_{2}}
and the first paragraph showed that
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{k^{s}})^{C_{2}}\simeq\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}
, so we conclude by observing that Lemma
6.12
says that

\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}_{0}(2)_{k})\to\coker\!\left[\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{k})^{S_{3}}\to\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br({Y}(2)_{k})^{C_{2}}\right]\cong\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{H}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}H(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\oplus\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k^{s}}

is split surjective and that
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 4.48613pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}{\mathop{}\kern 3.90283pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\Br}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{2}}}\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
is a direct summand of
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
.
∎

Notation 8.4
.

For a field
k
, and a scheme or stack
{X}/k
, we set

\Br_{1}{X}\coloneqq\ker\!\left(\Br{X}\to\Br{X}_{k^{s}}\right).

As a consequence of Lemma
8.3
, if
k
is a perfect field of characteristic not
2
, we have
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\cong\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
. By Lemma
8.2
, for any field
k
, we also have an exact sequence

(31)

0\longrightarrow\Br(k)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\longrightarrow\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\longrightarrow H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z)\xrightarrow{d}H^{3}(k,\mathbx G_{m})\oplus H^{3}(k,\mathbx Z).

In what follows, we further analyze this exact sequence in order to completely determine
\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
.

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
k
be any field of characteristic not
2
. The homomorphism
d
in (
31
) is zero and there exists a retraction
\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\to\Br(k)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)
. Thus,

\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\simeq\Br(k)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z).

If
k
is perfect, then we further have

\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\simeq\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\simeq\Br(k)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z)\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z.

Proof.

Recall
X_{k}=\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0,1\}
and
Y_{0}(2)_{k}=\mathbx A^{1}_{k}\setminus\{0\}
. Let
X_{k}\to{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
be the morphism corresponding to the Legendre family (with
(0,0)
as the chosen 2-torsion point), and let
{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\to Y_{0}(2)_{k}
be the coarse space map of Corollary
3.15
. To ease notation in this argument, write
\overline{k}\coloneqq k^{s}
for the separable closure, and let
G=\Gal(\overline{k}/k)
. The maps
X_{k}\to{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\to Y_{0}(2)_{k}
induce morphisms between their corresponding Hochschild-Serre spectral sequences, and so induce the following homomorphisms between the induced exact sequences of low degree terms:

(32)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G,\overline{k}[t,t^{-1},1/(t-1)]^{\times})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{1}X_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(G,\overline{k}[t,t^{-1},1/(t-1)]^{\times})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br(k)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(k,\mathbx G_{m})\oplus H^{3}(k,\mathbx Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G,\overline{k}[s,s^{-1}]^{\times})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{1}Y_{0}(2)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(G,\overline{k}[s,s^{-1}]^{\times}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

By construction, the maps
H^{i}(G,\overline{k}[s,s^{-1}]^{\times})\to H^{i}(G,\overline{k}[t,t^{-1},1/(t-1)]^{\times})
, for
i=2,3
, going from the bottom row to the top row above are simply the maps on
H^{i}(G,-)
induced by
s\mapsto t/(t-1)^{2}
. Identifying
\overline{k}[s,s^{-1}]^{\times}\simeq\overline{k}^{\times}\oplus sZ\simeq\overline{k}^{\times}\oplus\mathbx Z
and
\overline{k}[t,t^{-1},1/(t-1)]^{\times}\simeq\overline{k}^{\times}\oplus tZ\oplus(t-1)Z\simeq\overline{k}^{\times}\oplus\mathbx Z^{2}
, the map
s\mapsto t/(t-1)^{2}
is identified with the map

\begin{matrix}\displaystyle f:&\displaystyle\overline{k}^{\times}\oplus\mathbx Z&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\overline{k}^{\times}\oplus\mathbx Z^{2}\\
&\displaystyle(\alpha,n)&\longmapsto&\displaystyle(\alpha,n,-2n).\end{matrix}

This map is visibly a section of the projection
g\colon(\beta,a,b)\mapsto(\beta,a)
. Write
g\colon\overline{k}[t,t^{-1},1/(t-1)]^{\times}\to\overline{k}[s,s^{-1}]
for the corresponding map on unit groups. Commutativity of the diagram now gives us the following two conclusions:

•

d\colon H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z)\to H^{3}(k,\mathbx G_{m})\oplus H^{3}(k,\mathbx Z)
is the zero morphism.

Indeed,
\varphi\colon H^{3}(k,\mathbx G_{m})\oplus H^{3}(k,\mathbx Z)\simeq H^{3}(G,\overline{k}[s,s^{-1}]^{\times})\xrightarrow{f_{*}}H^{3}(G,\overline{k}[t,t^{-1},1/(t-1)]^{\times})
is split by
g_{*}\colon H^{3}(G,\overline{k}[t,t^{-1},1/(t-1)]^{\times})\to H^{3}(G,\overline{k}[s,s^{-1}])
and so injective. Since
\varphi
is injectivity, commutativity of the top right square of (
32
) shows that
d=0
.

•

The map
\Br(k)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\to\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}
appearing in the middle row of (
32
) is a split injection.

Indeed, commutativity of (
32
) along with the previous discussion shows that it is split by the composition

\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\to\Br X_{k}\simeq H^{2}(G,\overline{k}[t,t^{-1},(t-1)]^{\times})\xrightarrow{g_{*}}H^{2}(G,\overline{k}[s,s^{-1}]^{\times})\simeq\Br(k)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z).

Taken together, these show that

\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\simeq\Br(k)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z).

Finally, if
k
is perfect, then Lemma
8.3
shows that
\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\simeq\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z
.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

In the course of proving Theorem
8.5
, we showed that
\Br_{1}Y_{0}(2)_{k}\simeq H^{2}(G,\overline{k}[s,s^{-1}]^{\times})\simeq\Br(k)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Q/\mathbx Z)
. Thus, the conclusion of Theorem
8.5
can equivalently be written as

\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\simeq\Br_{1}Y_{0}(2)_{k}\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z)

for any field
k
of characteristic not
2
. If
k
is furthermore perfect, then Tsen’s theorem shows that
\Br_{1}Y_{0}(2)_{k}\simeq\Br Y_{0}(2)_{k}
and we have

\Br{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\simeq\Br_{1}{Y}_{0}(2)_{k}\oplus\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z\simeq\Br Y_{0}(2)_{k}\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z)\oplus H^{1}(k,\mathbx Z/2\mathbx Z).

Appendix A
Computing
\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})

In Section
7
, we need to know
\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})
when
S=\operatorname{Spec}k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic not
2
. In
[
Nil16
]
, this Picard group was computed for any
S/\mathbx Z[1/6]
but it was not computed in characteristic
3
. In contrast, the main results of
[
Lop23
]
apply over any field, and so could be used to compute
\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})
in the cases we need. Still, in the interest of plugging a gap in the literature, we show in this appendix that the ideas going into
[
Nil16
,
Lop23
]
apply just as well to compute
\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})
for any
S/\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
.

Recall A.1
.

Corollary
3.15
constructed a coarse space map
c=c_{S}:{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}\to Y_{0}(2)_{S}:=\mathbx A^{1}_{S}\setminus\{0\}
.

Notation A.2
.

Let
S
be a
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-scheme, and let
\pi=\pi_{S}:{E}\to{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}
denote the universal family over
{Y}_{0}(2)
. We write
\lambda=\lambda_{S}:=\pi_{*}\Omega^{1}_{{E}/{Y}_{0}(2)}
to denote the Hodge bundle on
{Y}_{0}(2)
. Note that, if
f_{S}:{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}\longrightarrow{Y}_{0}(2)
denotes the natural projection, then
\lambda_{S}\simeq f^{*}_{S}\lambda_{\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]}
.

The main theorem of this appendix is the below extension of
[
Nil16
, Theorem 2.4]
from
\mathbx Z[1/6]
-schemes to
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-schemes.

Theorem A.3
.

Let
S
be a
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-scheme. Then, the map

\begin{matrix}&\displaystyle\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z\times\Pic(Y_{0}(2)_{S})&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})\\
&\displaystyle(n,L)&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\lambda^{\otimes n}\otimes c^{*}L\end{matrix}

is an isomorphism.

Remark A.4
.

In the setting of this paper, when
S
is noetherian and regular,
\Pic(Y_{0}(2)_{S})=\Pic(S)
, so we have an isomorphism
\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z\times\Pic(S)\cong\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})
in this case.

We first show that the map in Theorem
A.3
is well-defined. For this, we will make use of the following fact.

Proposition A.5

(
[
Ols12
, Proposition 6.2]
)
.

Let
S
be a scheme,
{X}
a tame stack over
S
, with coarse moduli space
\pi:{X}\to X
. The pullback functor

\pi^{*}:(\text{line bundles on }X)\to(\text{line bundles on }{X})

induces an equivalence of categories between line bundles on
X
and line bundles
{L}
on
{X}
such that for all geometric points
x:\Spec k\to{X}
, the representation of
G_{x}
corresponding to
x^{*}{L}
is trivial.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
S
be a
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-scheme. Then, the Hodge bundle
\lambda=\lambda_{S}
is 4-torsion, i.e.
\lambda^{\otimes 4}\simeq O
on
{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}
.

Proof.

As remarked in Notation
A.2
,
\lambda_{S}
is the pullback of the Hodge bundle on
{Y}_{0}(2)
, so we may assume that
S=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
. Lemma
3.14
shows that
{Y}_{0}(2)
is a tame
\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}]
-stack and furthermore it shows that all its geometric points have automorphism groups of order dividing
4
. Thus,
\lambda^{\otimes 4}
induces the trivial representation at any geometric point of
{Y}_{0}(2)
, so Proposition
A.5
shows that
\lambda^{\otimes 4}\simeq c^{*}M
for some line bundle
M
on
Y_{0}(2)
. Finally,
Y_{0}(2)=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbx Z[\frac{1}{2}][s,s^{-1}]
has trivial Picard group, so
M
(and hence
\lambda^{\otimes 4}
) is trivial.
∎

We can now prove the main result of this appendix.

Proof of Theorem
A.3
.

In brief, once one has Lemma
A.6
showing that the map in Theorem
A.3
is well-defined, the rest of the proof goes through exactly as in
[
Nil16
]
. For the sake of completeness, we include details below.

Let
x:B\mu_{4,S}\to{Y}_{0}(2))_{S}
be the morphism corresponding to the elliptic curve
E:y^{2}=x^{3}-x
equipped with the point
P=(0,0)
of exact order
2
(this has automorphism group scheme
\mu_{4}
, where
\zeta\in\mu_{4}
acts via
(x,y)\mapsto(\zeta^{2}x,\zeta y)
). Let
\widetilde{x}:S\to{Y}_{0}(2)
be the restriction of
x
along
S\to B\mu_{4,S}
. If
L
is a line bundle on
{Y}_{0}(2)_{S}
, then
\widetilde{x}^{*}L=:M
is a line bundle on
S
with an action by
\mu_{4}
. Because
\underline{\Aut}(M)=\mathbx G_{m}
, this action corresponds to some representation
\chi:\mu_{4}\to\mathbx G_{m}
. Identifying
\underline{\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z}\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\mu_{4},\mathbx G_{m})
in the natural way, we see that pullback along
x
produces a map
f:\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})\to\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
.
One can check that
f(\lambda)=1\in\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z
by arguing as in
[
FO10
, Lemma 2.5]
, so
f
is surjective and split by the Hodge bundle, i.e.
\Pic({Y}_{0}(2)_{S})\simeq\mathbx Z/4\mathbx Z\oplus\ker f
. It remains to identify
\ker f\simeq c^{*}\Pic(Y_{0}(2)_{S})
. Say
L\in\ker f
. Then,
L
induces the trivial representation when restricted to
x
. However, by Lemma
7.4
, away from
x
every other geometric point of
{Y}_{0}(2)
has
\{\pm 1\}
as its automorphism group. Since
L\in\ker f
,
-1
acts trivially on the fiber of
L
above any point with automorphism group
\mu_{4}
, but one can argue as in
[
FO10
, Corollary 2.4]
to show that this means that
-1
acts trivially on the fiber of
L
over any geometric point. Thus,
L\in c^{*}\Pic(Y_{0}(2)_{S})
by Proposition
A.5
, finishing the proof.
∎
